# The Lorentz Super-Loop of the Bi-Octonions: Non-Associative Counterexamples to the No-Go
Theorems and a Spin-Zero Spinor Superfluid

**Patrick Lee Nash**  
*PROJECT spacetimes*  
November 16, 2026

> Markdown version generated from `The Lorentz Super-Loop of the Bi-Octonions - Non-Associative Counterexamples to the No-Go Theorems and a Spin-Zero Spinor Superfluid.tex` by `code/scripts/tex2md.py`; the typeset PDF (`The Lorentz Super-Loop of the Bi-Octonions - Non-Associative Counterexamples to the No-Go Theorems and a Spin-Zero Spinor Superfluid.pdf`) is authoritative for layout. ## Abstract

The theorems of McGlinn, O’Raifeartaigh, Coleman and Mandula, and Haag, Łopuszański and Sohnius forbid a nontrivial union of Lorentz and internal symmetry. Each assumes that symmetries compose associatively: they form a group, act by operators, and their generators obey the Jacobi identity. We drop associativity. The unit bi-octonions form a Moufang loop, the Lorentz super-loop, that contains $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ and eight spinor charges, a Weyl spinor that is an isodoublet with electric charges $\pm\tfrac12$, whose brackets close into the Lorentz generators. We prove that no Lie algebra does this, that the super-loop’s Malcev algebra is the unique non-associative solution, and that it has no realization by operators. We identify the manifolds that carry it and analyze the spin-zero superfluid of its spinor charges: isospin-triplet pairs win, and a full super-loop singlet needs two species. The four-dimensional theory has a renormalizable Yukawa form; in eight dimensions it is non-renormalizable. All 74 statements are machine-checked.

## I. Introduction

A *symmetry* of a physical system is a transformation that maps solutions (or states) to solutions and leaves the laws unchanged. Two kinds are familiar. *Space-time* symmetries act on the coordinates: rotations, boosts (together the Lorentz transformations) and translations. *Internal* symmetries act on the components of the fields at each point and commute with every space-time transformation, like the isospin rotations of a doublet of fields or the phase rotations whose generator is the electric charge. In the 1960s it was hoped that the two kinds could be united in one larger symmetry, in which an internal multiplet would also be a Lorentz multiplet. A series of theorems—McGlinn ([McGlinn 1964](#ref-McGlinn1964)), O’Raifeartaigh ([O’Raifeartaigh 1965](#ref-ORaifeartaigh1965)) and Coleman and Mandula ([Coleman and Mandula 1967](#ref-ColemanMandula1967))—showed that this is impossible under hypotheses that seemed harmless, and Haag, Łopuszański and Sohnius ([Haag, Łopuszański, and Sohnius 1975](#ref-HLS1975)) showed that the only way out within operator algebras is to admit generators that *anticommute*: supersymmetry. Pelc and Horwitz extended the theorem of Coleman and Mandula to any number of space dimensions ([Pelc and Horwitz 1997](#ref-PelcHorwitz1997)).

A theorem cannot have a counterexample in which all its hypotheses hold. What can exist is a structure in which a hypothesis fails and the conclusion of the theorem fails with it: a *counterexample to the conclusion*. This paper constructs such structures by giving up one hypothesis that every proof uses and none states as a physical assumption: *associativity*. The symmetries are assumed to compose like the elements of a group (associatively: three transformations performed in succession give a result that does not depend on which two are combined first), to act by linear operators, whose products are associative, and therefore to have generators that obey the Jacobi identity. We show in Sec. II exactly where these assumptions enter.

The non-associative object we use is the algebra of *bi-octonions* $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$, the octonions with complex coefficients. Its unit elements form a *Moufang loop*, a set with a product that has a unit element and inverses but is not associative, which we call the *Lorentz super-loop* $\mathbb L_{\rm LL}$. It contains the group $SL(2,\mathbb C)$, the double cover of the Lorentz group, and eight further directions, the *spinor charges*, which transform as a Weyl spinor of one chirality and form an isospin doublet with electric charges $\pm\tfrac12$. In its algebra of infinitesimal elements, a Malcev algebra $\mathfrak m_{\rm LL}$, two spinor charges have a Lorentz generator as their bracket. We prove (Theorem L) that no Lie algebra can do this, that the super-loop is the only solution among all Lorentz- and isospin-covariant brackets (Theorem M), and that its non-associativity is irreducible: it has no realization by operators (Theorem R). These are the requested counterexamples: Lorentz and internal degrees of freedom combined in one structure, with operators whose eigenvalues are the electric charge, the chirality, the spinor number, and a new quantity, the *twist charge*, which takes the value $-2$ on the spinor charges and measures by how much the Lorentz action on them fails to be a representation.

We then ask which manifolds carry the super-loop (Sec. VII): the Minkowski spaces of $7{+}1$ and $3+1$ dimensions inside the bi-octonions, the split octonions of signature $4{+}4$, the seven-sphere and the super-loop itself, a parallelizable manifold that is not a Lie group. We analyze the spin-zero superfluid of the spinor charges (Sec. IX): the field content follows from the bi-octonion product, the spin-zero pairs of one field are isospin triplets, the unitary state wins, and the pair that is a singlet of the whole super-loop needs two species of fields. Finally we decide whether these theories are renormalizable (Sec. X).

Every statement of this paper is verified by a machine check, labelled as in Appendix B (for example A-2 or S-5); exact statements are checked in exact integer or rational arithmetic. The programs, the data files and the commands that reproduce every number are listed in Appendix C, and every symbol is defined in Appendix A. Units: $\hbar$ and the speed of light are 1; in Secs. IX and X the momentum cutoff $\Lambda_{\rm LL}=1$ unless a unit is shown.

## II. The no-go theorems and where associativity enters

### A. What the theorems say

A *generator* of a continuous symmetry is the infinitesimal transformation: the derivative, at the identity, of a one-parameter family of transformations. The generators of a group of transformations form a vector space with a *bracket*, the generator of the commutator of two infinitesimal transformations; for a Lie group this is a *Lie algebra*: the bracket is bilinear, antisymmetric, and obeys the Jacobi identity, i.e. its *Jacobiator* $$\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x,\mathbf y,\mathbf z)=[[\mathbf x,\mathbf y],\mathbf z]+[[\mathbf y,\mathbf z],\mathbf x]+[[\mathbf z,\mathbf x],\mathbf y]
\tag{1}$$ vanishes for all generators $\mathbf x$, $\mathbf y$, $\mathbf z$. (In this section $\mathbf x$, $\mathbf y$, $\mathbf z$ stand for generators; from Sec. III on they are bi-octonions.) The Lorentz algebra $\mathfrak{so}(3,1)$ has six generators, three rotations and three boosts; adding the four translations gives the Poincaré algebra.

The no-go theorems state, under hypotheses listed below, that a symmetry algebra containing the Poincaré algebra and an internal algebra is their direct sum:

- McGlinn ([McGlinn 1964](#ref-McGlinn1964)): in a Lie algebra that contains the Poincaré algebra and an internal algebra, and in which the translations act nilpotently, the internal generators commute with the Poincaré generators.

- O’Raifeartaigh ([O’Raifeartaigh 1965](#ref-ORaifeartaigh1965)): if a finite-dimensional Lie algebra contains the Poincaré algebra and acts by a unitary representation in which the squared mass has an isolated eigenvalue, the members of a multiplet all have the same mass; the algebra cannot split masses.

- Coleman and Mandula ([Coleman and Mandula 1967](#ref-ColemanMandula1967)) (in any number of space dimensions with one time, Pelc and Horwitz ([Pelc and Horwitz 1997](#ref-PelcHorwitz1997))): the most general Lie group of symmetries of a non-trivial scattering matrix of a relativistic theory with finitely many particles below any mass, analytic amplitudes and a mass gap is, locally, the direct product of the Poincaré group and a compact internal group.

- Haag, Łopuszański and Sohnius ([Haag, Łopuszański, and Sohnius 1975](#ref-HLS1975)) (see also ([Weinberg 2000](#ref-WeinbergQFT3))): if the generators are allowed to form a *Lie superalgebra*, in which some generators (called odd) have an anticommutator instead of a commutator, the odd generators are spinors whose anticommutators are translations (and central charges): supersymmetry.

### B. The hypotheses

We label the hypotheses that the proofs use:

- **H-grp.** The symmetry transformations form a group: their composition is associative, there is an identity, and every transformation has an inverse.

- **H-op.** They act on the states by linear (unitary or antiunitary) operators, so that the composition of transformations is the product of operators; particles are irreducible unitary representations of the Poincaré group ([Wigner 1939](#ref-Wigner1939)).

- **H-Lie.** The group is a Lie group; its generators form a Lie algebra.

- **H-gr.** (Haag, Łopuszański and Sohnius only) The generators form a Lie superalgebra, whose graded Jacobi identity is the Jacobi identity with the signs of the anticommutators.

- **H-dyn.** The dynamical hypotheses on the scattering matrix: it is non-trivial, the amplitudes are analytic, there are finitely many particles below any mass, and there is a mass gap.

Every theorem above uses H-Lie (or H-gr), and the theorems on the scattering matrix use H-op and H-dyn as well.

### C. Where associativity enters

H-grp states associativity. H-op implies it, because the product of operators is associative. And H-Lie follows from either. Take any three elements $\mathsf O_1$, $\mathsf O_2$, $\mathsf O_3$ of an algebra (for example operators), their commutator $[\mathsf O_1,\mathsf O_2]=\mathsf O_1\mathsf
O_2-\mathsf O_2\mathsf O_1$, and their *associator* $[\mathsf O_1,\mathsf O_2,\mathsf O_3]=(\mathsf O_1\mathsf O_2)\mathsf O_3-\mathsf O_1(\mathsf O_2\mathsf O_3)$. Expanding the twelve products of the Jacobiator and sorting them by the order of the factors gives $$\begin{aligned}
&\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathsf O_1,\mathsf O_2,\mathsf O_3)\\
&\quad=[\mathsf O_1,\mathsf O_2,\mathsf O_3]+[\mathsf O_2,\mathsf O_3,\mathsf O_1]+[\mathsf O_3,\mathsf O_1,\mathsf O_2]\\
&\quad\phantom{=}-[\mathsf O_2,\mathsf O_1,\mathsf O_3]-[\mathsf O_1,\mathsf O_3,\mathsf O_2]-[\mathsf O_3,\mathsf O_2,\mathsf O_1]:
\tag{2}
\end{aligned}$$ the associators of the even orderings of the three elements minus those of the odd orderings. For an associative product (operators) every associator vanishes, so the Jacobi identity holds identically (check A-6 verifies Eq. (2) for random integer matrices). Equation (2) holds for any product, associative or not, and it is the hinge of this paper: in a non-associative algebra the commutator need not obey the Jacobi identity, and the generators of its symmetries need not form a Lie algebra.

### D. What a counterexample can be

A counterexample in which all hypotheses of a theorem hold does not exist; a search for one would end in an error. We therefore construct structures in which H-grp, H-op and H-Lie (and H-gr) fail *because associativity fails*, while Lorentz covariance is kept exactly, and in which the conclusion of the theorems—no non-trivial union of Lorentz and internal symmetry—fails. The dynamical hypotheses H-dyn concern the scattering matrix of a quantum theory; because our structures have no realization by operators (Theorem R, Sec. III), they cannot act on the states of an ordinary quantum theory, and H-dyn is not tested. A quantum mechanics over non-associative algebras has been studied since Jordan, von Neumann and Wigner ([Jordan, Neumann, and Wigner 1934](#ref-JordanNeumannWigner1934)) and, for the octonions, by Günaydin, Piron and Ruegg ([Günaydin, Piron, and Ruegg 1978](#ref-GunaydinPironRuegg1978)); we do not use it.

## III. The bi-octonions

### A. Quaternions and bi-quaternions

The quaternions $\mathbb H$ are the real combinations of four units $\mathbf e_0=1$, $\mathbf e_1$, $\mathbf e_2$, $\mathbf e_3$ with the associative product $$\mathbf e_j^2=-\mathbf e_0,\qquad \mathbf e_1\mathbf e_2=-\mathbf e_2\mathbf e_1=\mathbf e_3
\tag{3}$$ and its cyclic permutations ($\mathbf e_2\mathbf e_3=\mathbf e_1$, $\mathbf e_3\mathbf e_1=\mathbf e_2$). Allowing complex coefficients gives the *bi-quaternions* $\mathbb C\otimes\mathbb H$, whose elements we write $\mathbf a$, $\mathbf b$, $\mathbf c$, $\mathbf d$. The imaginary unit $i$ of the coefficients commutes with the units; it is not one of them. The map $M_{\rm LL}$ with $M_{\rm LL}(\mathbf e_0)=\sigma^{\rm LL}_0$ and $M_{\rm LL}(\mathbf e_j)=-i\sigma^{\rm LL}_j$ (the Pauli matrices, $j=1,2,3$), extended complex-linearly, is an isomorphism of $\mathbb C\otimes\mathbb H$ onto the complex $2\times2$ matrices: $M_{\rm LL}(\mathbf a\mathbf b)=M_{\rm LL}(\mathbf a)M_{\rm
LL}(\mathbf b)$. Under it the norm of a bi-quaternion, $N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf b)=\sum_p\xi^{\rm
LL}_p(\mathbf b)^2$ (the sum of the squares of its four complex coefficients, $p=0,\dots,3$), becomes the determinant, and the unit bi-quaternions become the group $SL(2,\mathbb C)$, the double cover of the Lorentz group of $3+1$ dimensions.

### B. Bi-octonions

The bi-octonions $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$, which have been used in particle physics before ([Günaydin and Gürsey 1973](#ref-GunaydinGursey1973); [Dixon 1994](#ref-Dixon1994); [Okubo 1995](#ref-Okubo1995); [Furey 2016](#ref-Furey2016)), are pairs of bi-quaternions, written $\mathbf x=\mathbf a+\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4$, with the Cayley-Dickson product $$(\mathbf a+\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4)(\mathbf c+\mathbf d\,\mathbf e_4)=(\mathbf a\mathbf c-\bar{\mathbf d}\mathbf b)+(\mathbf d\mathbf a+\mathbf b\bar{\mathbf c})\,\mathbf e_4 ,
\tag{4}$$ where the bar changes the signs of the coefficients of $\mathbf e_1$, $\mathbf e_2$, $\mathbf e_3$. The eight units are $\mathbf e_0,\dots,\mathbf e_3$, $\mathbf e_4$ and $\mathbf e_5=\mathbf e_1\mathbf e_4$, $\mathbf e_6=\mathbf e_2\mathbf e_4$, $\mathbf e_7=\mathbf e_3\mathbf e_4$; every bi-octonion is $\mathbf x=\sum_{p=0}^7\xi^{\rm LL}_p(\mathbf x)\,\mathbf e_p$ with complex coefficients, so $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$ has real dimension 16. The real combinations form the octonions $\mathbb O$. Figure 1(a) shows the product of the units. Three special cases of Eq. (4) are used throughout: $$\begin{aligned}
\mathbf a(\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4)&=(\mathbf b\mathbf a)\,\mathbf e_4,\qquad (\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4)\mathbf c=(\mathbf b\bar{\mathbf c})\,\mathbf e_4,\\
(\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4)(\mathbf d\,\mathbf e_4)&=-\bar{\mathbf d}\mathbf b .
\tag{5}
\end{aligned}$$ The *conjugation* $\bar{\mathbf x}$ changes the signs of the coefficients of $\mathbf e_1,\dots,\mathbf e_7$; it is complex-linear and reverses products, $\overline{\mathbf x\mathbf y}=\bar{\mathbf y}\bar{\mathbf x}$. The complex conjugation $\mathbf x^*$ conjugates every coefficient, and $\mathbf x^\dagger=(\bar{\mathbf x})^*$. The *norm* $N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x)=\sum_p\xi^{\rm LL}_p(\mathbf x)^2$ is a complex number with $\mathbf x\bar{\mathbf x}=N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x)\,\mathbf e_0$; its polarization is $N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x,\mathbf y)=\sum_p\xi^{\rm LL}_p(\mathbf x)\xi^{\rm LL}_p(\mathbf y)$.

![image](../figures/../figures/fig_sl_tables.pdf.png)

\(a\) The product $\mathbf e_p\mathbf e_{p'}$ of the units of $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$ (row: left factor; column: right factor; orange: a minus sign), from Eq. (4); the lines separate the quaternion units $\mathbf e_0,\dots,\mathbf e_3$ from $\mathbf e_4,\dots,\mathbf e_7$. (b) The unordered triples of distinct imaginary units, sorted by how many lie in the Lorentz part $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$ (units $\mathbf e_1$, $\mathbf e_2$, $\mathbf e_3$) and how many in the spinor charges $\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}$ (units $\mathbf e_4,\dots,\mathbf e_7$), and the number with a non-zero associator: 168 ordered triples of units in all (check A-2).

### C. Alternative, not associative

The *associator* $[\mathbf x,\mathbf y,\mathbf z]=(\mathbf x\mathbf y)\mathbf z-\mathbf x(\mathbf
y\mathbf z)$ measures the failure of associativity. For the bi-octonions:

1.  The norm is multiplicative, $N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x\mathbf y)=N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x)N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf
    y)$ (check A-3, exact on random bi-octonions with Gaussian-integer coefficients).

2.  The associator is *alternating*: it changes sign when two arguments are exchanged, and so vanishes when two arguments are equal (A-1). An algebra with this property is called *alternative*; in it any two elements generate an associative subalgebra (Artin’s theorem ([Schafer 1966](#ref-Schafer1966))).

3.  The product is not associative: 168 of the 512 ordered triples of units have a non-zero associator, the triples of three distinct imaginary units that do not lie in a common quaternion subalgebra; for example $[\mathbf e_1,\mathbf e_2,\mathbf e_4]=2\mathbf e_7$ (A-2). Figure 1(b) sorts them.

4.  The Moufang identities $\mathbf z(\mathbf x(\mathbf z\mathbf y))=((\mathbf z\mathbf x)\mathbf z)\mathbf y$, $((\mathbf y\mathbf z)\mathbf x)\mathbf z=\mathbf y(\mathbf z(\mathbf x\mathbf z))$ and $(\mathbf z\mathbf x)(\mathbf y\mathbf z)=(\mathbf z(\mathbf x\mathbf y))\mathbf z$ hold (A-3).

Because the associator is alternating, the six associators of Eq. (2) are all equal up to their signs, and the Jacobiator of the commutator is $$\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x,\mathbf y,\mathbf z)=6\,[\mathbf x,\mathbf y,\mathbf z]
\tag{6}$$ (A-4): the Jacobi identity fails exactly where associativity fails.

### D. The super-loop and its Malcev algebra

A *loop* is a set with a product, an identity element, and unique solutions of the equations $\mathbf w\mathbf x=\mathbf y$ and $\mathbf x\mathbf w=\mathbf y$ for $\mathbf x$; a group is an associative loop. A *Moufang loop* obeys the Moufang identities ([Moufang 1935](#ref-Moufang1935)). The unit bi-octonions $$\mathbb L_{\rm LL}=\{\mathbf w\in\mathbb B_{\rm LL}:\ N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf w)=1\}
\tag{7}$$ form a Moufang loop: the product of two of them is one of them (the norm is multiplicative), $\mathbf
e_0$ is the identity, $\bar{\mathbf w}$ is the inverse of $\mathbf w$, and $\mathbf w\mathbf x=\mathbf y$ has the unique solution $\mathbf x=\bar{\mathbf w}\mathbf y$, because $\bar{\mathbf w}(\mathbf w\mathbf
y)=(\bar{\mathbf w}\mathbf w)\mathbf y$ by alternativity. We call it the *Lorentz super-loop*. It is a complex manifold of complex dimension 7 (real dimension 14), and it contains $SL(2,\mathbb C)$, the unit bi-quaternions, as a subgroup.

Just as a Lie group has a Lie algebra, a Moufang loop that is a smooth manifold has a tangent algebra at the identity: the imaginary bi-octonions $\mathfrak m_{\rm LL}=\mathbb C\otimes{\rm Im}\,\mathbb O$ with the commutator (real dimension 14). It is not a Lie algebra, by Eq. (6), but it is a *Malcev algebra* ([Mal’cev 1955](#ref-Malcev1955); [Sagle 1961](#ref-Sagle1961)): its bracket is bilinear and antisymmetric and obeys the Malcev identity $\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x,\mathbf y,[\mathbf x,\mathbf z])=[\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm
LL}(\mathbf x,\mathbf y,\mathbf z),\mathbf x]$, which in a Lie algebra holds trivially. We verify it in the linearized form, obtained by replacing $\mathbf x$ by $\mathbf x+\mathbf w$ and keeping the part linear in $\mathbf w$, $$\begin{aligned}
&\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x,\mathbf y,[\mathbf w,\mathbf z])+\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf w,\mathbf y,[\mathbf x,\mathbf z])\\
&\quad=[\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x,\mathbf y,\mathbf z),\mathbf w]+[\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf w,\mathbf y,\mathbf z),\mathbf x],
\tag{8}
\end{aligned}$$ on all 2401 4-tuples of imaginary units (A-5). Malcev algebras are to analytic Moufang loops what Lie algebras are to Lie groups ([Mal’cev 1955](#ref-Malcev1955); [Kuz’min 1971](#ref-Kuzmin1971); [Nagy and Strambach 2002](#ref-NagyStrambach2002)).

### E. No realization by operators

*Theorem R.* (a) Every linear map $\varrho_{\rm LL}$ from $\mathfrak m_{\rm LL}$ to the linear operators on a vector space that turns brackets into commutators, $\varrho_{\rm LL}([\mathbf x,\mathbf y])=\varrho_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x)\varrho_{\rm LL}(\mathbf
y)-\varrho_{\rm LL}(\mathbf y)\varrho_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x)$, is zero. (b) No one-to-one map of $\mathbb L_{\rm LL}$ into the invertible operators on a vector space turns the product of the loop into the product of operators.

*Proof.* (a) Applying $\varrho_{\rm LL}$ to Eq. (1) gives $\varrho_{\rm LL}(\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x,\mathbf y,\mathbf z))=\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm
LL}(\varrho_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x),\varrho_{\rm LL}(\mathbf y),\varrho_{\rm LL}(\mathbf z))$, and the right side vanishes by Eq. (2), because the product of operators is associative (A-6). Hence $\varrho_{\rm LL}$ vanishes on every Jacobiator. The Jacobiators of the real basis $\mathbf e_p$, $i\mathbf e_p$ ($p=1,\dots,7$) of $\mathfrak m_{\rm LL}$ span a space of real dimension 14, all of $\mathfrak m_{\rm LL}$ (A-8); so $\varrho_{\rm LL}=0$. (b) Such a map would send $(\mathbf e_1\mathbf e_2)\mathbf e_4=\mathbf e_7$ and $\mathbf e_1(\mathbf e_2\mathbf e_4)=-\mathbf
e_7$ to the same operator, because both products of operators are equal; it would not be one-to-one. $\square$

In particular the left multiplications $L^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf x}:\mathbf y\mapsto\mathbf x\mathbf y$, real $16\times16$ matrices, do not represent $\mathfrak m_{\rm LL}$: $[L^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf e_1},L^{\rm
LL}_{\mathbf e_4}]\neq L^{\rm LL}_{[\mathbf e_1,\mathbf e_4]}$ (A-7). Theorem R is the precise sense in which the non-associativity is essential: no reformulation by operators, and so no Hilbert space of states on which the super-loop acts linearly and faithfully, removes it. Hypotheses H-grp, H-op and H-Lie fail for $\mathbb L_{\rm LL}$, and they fail together.

## IV. The Lorentz super-loop

### A. Lorentz part and spinor charges

The Malcev algebra splits into two parts, $\mathfrak m_{\rm LL}=\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}\oplus\mathfrak s_{\rm
LL}$. The *Lorentz part* $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}=\mathbb C\otimes{\rm Im}\,\mathbb H$ consists of the imaginary bi-quaternions $\mathbf u$ (real dimension 6). With the rotation and boost generators $$J^j_{\rm LL}=\tfrac12\mathbf e_j,\qquad K^j_{\rm LL}=\tfrac12 i\,\mathbf e_j\qquad(j=1,2,3)
\tag{9}$$ Eq. (3) gives the brackets of the Lorentz algebra $\mathfrak{so}(3,1)$ (check L-1): $$\begin{aligned}
&=\textstyle\sum_{j''}\epsilon^{\rm LL}_{jj'j''}J^{j''}_{\rm LL},\\
[J^j_{\rm LL},K^{j'}_{\rm LL}]&=\textstyle\sum_{j''}\epsilon^{\rm LL}_{jj'j''}K^{j''}_{\rm LL},\tag{10}\\
[K^j_{\rm LL},K^{j'}_{\rm LL}]&=-\textstyle\sum_{j''}\epsilon^{\rm LL}_{jj'j''}J^{j''}_{\rm LL}.
\end{aligned}$$ Its Killing form $\mathrm{Kil}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x,\mathbf y)=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{ad}\,\mathbf
x\,\mathrm{ad}\,\mathbf y)$ has three negative (rotations) and three positive (boosts) eigenvalues (L-2; $\mathrm{Kil}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf e_1,\mathbf e_1)={}$-16, $\mathrm{Kil}_{\rm LL}(i\mathbf e_1,i\mathbf e_1)={}$<!-- -->16), as for $\mathfrak{so}(3,1)$. $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$ is a Lie subalgebra, the tangent algebra of the subgroup $SL(2,\mathbb C)$.

The *spinor charges* are the elements $\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4$ of $\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}=(\mathbb
C\otimes\mathbb H)\,\mathbf e_4$ (real dimension 8). By Eq. (5), $\mathbf u(\mathbf
b\mathbf e_4)=(\mathbf b\mathbf u)\mathbf e_4$ and $(\mathbf b\mathbf e_4)\mathbf u=(\mathbf b\bar{\mathbf
u})\mathbf e_4=-(\mathbf b\mathbf u)\mathbf e_4$, so a Lorentz element acts on a spinor charge by $$=2(\mathbf b\mathbf u)\,\mathbf e_4=-2\,(W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u)\mathbf b)\,\mathbf e_4,
\tag{11}$$ where $W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u)\mathbf b=-\mathbf b\mathbf u$. The map $W_{\rm LL}$ is a representation of $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$, $[W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u_1),W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u_2)]=W_{\rm LL}([\mathbf u_1,\mathbf u_2])$, because $W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u_1)W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u_2)\mathbf b=\mathbf b\mathbf u_2\mathbf u_1$. It is the *Weyl spinor*: under $M_{\rm LL}$ the right multiplication by $\mathbf u$ acts on the rows of the $2\times2$ matrix $M_{\rm LL}(\mathbf b)$, and $W_{\rm LL}(J^j_{\rm LL})$, $W_{\rm LL}(K^j_{\rm LL})$ act on each row as the generators of the $(\tfrac12,0)$ representation (Sec. IX). The two rows are an *isospin doublet*: left multiplication by a unit quaternion $\mathbf v$, $\mathbf b\mapsto\mathbf v\mathbf b$, mixes them and commutes with $W_{\rm LL}$. The inner action $\rho_{\rm LL}=-2W_{\rm LL}$ of Eq. (11) is *not* a representation: $[\rho_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u_1),\rho_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u_2)]=4W_{\rm LL}([\mathbf u_1,\mathbf u_2])=-2\rho_{\rm
LL}([\mathbf u_1,\mathbf u_2])$ (L-3).

Two spinor charges have, by Eq. (5), the bracket $$=\bar{\mathbf b}\mathbf b'-\bar{\mathbf b}'\mathbf b\ \in\ \mathfrak l_{\rm LL},
\tag{12}$$ an imaginary bi-quaternion, i.e. a Lorentz generator; these brackets span all of $\mathfrak l_{\rm
LL}$ (L-4). Spinor charges close into Lorentz generators. Figure 2 summarizes the structure.

![image](../figures/../figures/fig_sl_superloop.pdf.png)

The Malcev algebra of the Lorentz super-loop: its Lorentz part $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$ and its spinor charges $\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}$, their brackets, Eqs. (11) and (12), and the outer operators of Sec. V, which act on the loop by automorphisms. The labels are those of the machine checks.

### B. The twist charge and the Jacobiators

Define the *twist charge* $\Theta_{\rm LL}$ as the linear map of $\mathfrak m_{\rm LL}$ that is $1$ on $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$ and $-2$ on $\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}$. With the Lorentz derivation $D_{\rm
LL}(\mathbf u)$ of Sec. V, which acts on $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$ by the commutator and on $\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}$ by $W_{\rm LL}$, the inner action factors as $[\mathbf u,\cdot\,]=D_{\rm
LL}(\mathbf u)\circ\Theta_{\rm LL}$ (L-7). Suppose more generally that $\mathbf u$ acts on the spinor charges by $\Theta_{\rm LL}W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u)$ with any number $\Theta_{\rm LL}$. Then $$\begin{aligned}
&\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u_1,\mathbf u_2,\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4)\\
&=\Big(\big(\Theta_{\rm LL}W_{\rm LL}([\mathbf u_1,\mathbf u_2])\\
&\qquad-\Theta_{\rm LL}^2[W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u_1),W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u_2)]\big)\mathbf b\Big)\,\mathbf e_4\\
&=(\Theta_{\rm LL}-\Theta_{\rm LL}^2)\,(W_{\rm LL}([\mathbf u_1,\mathbf u_2])\mathbf b)\,\mathbf e_4 ,
\tag{13}
\end{aligned}$$ which vanishes for the Lie actions $\Theta_{\rm LL}=0$ (trivial) and $\Theta_{\rm LL}=1$ (the Weyl representation) and equals $-6(W_{\rm LL}([\mathbf u_1,\mathbf u_2])\mathbf b)\mathbf e_4$ for the super-loop, $\Theta_{\rm LL}=-2$ (L-5, exact on all 288 basis triples). The Jacobiators of three spinor charges and of one Lorentz element with two spinor charges are non-zero as well, for example $\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf e_4,\mathbf e_5,\mathbf e_6)=-12\,\mathbf e_7$ (L-5).

### C. Theorem L: no Lie algebra does this

*Theorem L.* Let $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}\oplus\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}$ carry a bracket that is the Lorentz bracket on $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$, acts on $\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}$ covariantly, $[\mathbf
u,\mathbf b\mathbf e_4]=c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}(W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u)\mathbf b)\mathbf e_4$, and has $[\mathbf b\mathbf e_4,\mathbf b'\mathbf e_4]=c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}(\bar{\mathbf b}\mathbf b'-\bar{\mathbf
b}'\mathbf b)$, with complex numbers $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}$, $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}$. (Section VI shows that these are the most general Lorentz- and isospin-covariant brackets.) If the Jacobi identity holds, then $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}=0$: in a Lie algebra, bosonic spinor charges cannot close into the Lorentz generators.

*Proof.* By Eq. (13) with $\Theta_{\rm LL}=c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}$, the Jacobi identity on $(\mathbf u_1,\mathbf u_2,\mathbf b\mathbf e_4)$ requires $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}=0$ or $1$. If $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}=0$, the Jacobi identity on $(\mathbf u,\mathbf b\mathbf e_4,\mathbf b'\mathbf
e_4)$ reduces to $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}[\bar{\mathbf b}\mathbf b'-\bar{\mathbf b}'\mathbf b,\mathbf u]=0$ for every $\mathbf u$; the brackets span $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$ (L-4), whose center is zero, so $c^{\rm
sl}_{\rm LL}=0$. If $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}=1$, the Jacobi identity on three spinor charges reads $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}\sum_{\rm cyc}(W_{\rm LL}(\bar{\mathbf b}\mathbf b'-\bar{\mathbf b}'\mathbf b)\mathbf
b'')\mathbf e_4=0$, the sum running over the three cyclic orders of $(\mathbf b,\mathbf b',\mathbf b'')$. For $\mathbf b=\mathbf e_0$, $\mathbf b'=\mathbf e_1$, $\mathbf b''=\mathbf e_2$ the three brackets are $2\mathbf e_1$, $-2\mathbf e_3$, $-2\mathbf e_2$, and the sum is $6c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}\mathbf e_7$; so $c^{\rm
sl}_{\rm LL}=0$. $\square$

Check L-6 verifies the last step on all basis triples, and the Gröbner-basis classification of Sec. VI confirms the theorem for all coefficients and extends it to the Poincaré algebra and to an added isospin algebra (G-1, G-5, G-10). The same computation with $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}=-2$, $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}=1$ gives $\mathrm{Jac}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf e_4,\mathbf
e_5,\mathbf e_6)=6c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}\,\mathbf e_7=-12\,\mathbf e_7$, the value of L-5: the super-loop violates the Jacobi identity, but it obeys the Malcev identity.

## V. Operators on the super-loop and their eigenvalues

### A. Derivations and automorphisms

An *automorphism* of $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$ is an invertible linear map that preserves the product; a *derivation* is its infinitesimal form, a linear map $\mathcal D_{\rm LL}$ with $\mathcal D_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x\mathbf y)=(\mathcal D_{\rm LL}\mathbf x)\mathbf y+\mathbf x(\mathcal
D_{\rm LL}\mathbf y)$. Automorphisms map $\mathbb L_{\rm LL}$ to itself, so they are symmetries of the super-loop that act on it from outside; they are linear operators on $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$ and, unlike the elements of the loop, compose associatively. Two families matter (L-7): $$\begin{aligned}
D_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u)(\mathbf a+\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4)&=[\mathbf u,\mathbf a]+(-\mathbf b\mathbf u)\,\mathbf e_4,\tag{14}\\
D^{\rm v}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf t)(\mathbf a+\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4)&=(\mathbf t\mathbf b)\,\mathbf e_4,\tag{15}
\end{aligned}$$ for a Lorentz element $\mathbf u\in\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$ and an isospin element $\mathbf t\in{\rm
Im}\,\mathbb H$. Both are derivations (checked on random pairs), they commute with each other, and the isospin derivations vanish on $\mathbb C\otimes\mathbb H$. Integrated, they give the automorphisms $$\varphi^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf g,\mathbf v}(\mathbf a+\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4)=\mathbf g\mathbf a\bar{\mathbf g}+(\mathbf v\mathbf b\bar{\mathbf g})\,\mathbf e_4
\tag{16}$$ for unit bi-quaternions $\mathbf g$ (a Lorentz transformation) and $\mathbf v$ (an isospin rotation) (L-9); together they form $(SL(2,\mathbb C)\times SL(2,\mathbb C))/\mathbb Z_2=SO(4,\mathbb C)$. All derivations of $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$ form the complex exceptional Lie algebra $\mathfrak g_2(\mathbb
C)$, of real dimension 28: the linear conditions for a derivation have rank 228 for the 256 entries of a real $16\times16$ matrix, and the inner derivations $[L^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf x},L^{\rm
LL}_{\mathbf y}]+[L^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf x},R^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf y}]+[R^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf x},R^{\rm
LL}_{\mathbf y}]$ (with the right multiplications $R^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf y}$) span 28 dimensions (L-8). The automorphism group is $\mathrm G_2(\mathbb C)$, and $SO(4,\mathbb C)$ is the part of it that keeps $\mathbb C\otimes\mathbb H$ in place.

### B. The operators and their eigenvalues

The super-loop carries the following operators, all built from Eqs. (14) and (15); their eigenvalues are collected in Fig. 3.

*Isospin and electric charge.* The isospin generators $T^\iota_{\rm LL}=i\,D^{\rm v}_{\rm
LL}(\mathbf e_\iota/2)$ ($\iota=1,2,3$) are Hermitian and obey $[T^1_{\rm LL},T^2_{\rm LL}]=iT^3_{\rm
LL}$ and its cyclic permutations. We call the third one the *electric charge*, $Q_{\rm LL}=T^3_{\rm LL}$. It has the eigenvalue 0 on $\mathbb C\otimes\mathbb H$ (four times) and the eigenvalues $+\tfrac12$ and $-\tfrac12$ (twice each) on the spinor charges (Q-1); the isospin Casimir $(T_{\rm LL})^2=\sum_\iota(T^\iota_{\rm LL})^2$ is 0 on $\mathbb C\otimes\mathbb H$ and $\tfrac34=\tfrac12(\tfrac12+1)$ on the spinor charges, which are therefore an isospin doublet (Q-2).

*Spin.* The Casimir of the rotations, $\mathrm{Cas}_{\rm LL}=-\sum_jD_{\rm LL}(J^j_{\rm LL})^2$, has the eigenvalue 0 on $\mathbf e_0$ (spin 0), 2 on the Lorentz part (spin 1) and $\tfrac34$ on the spinor charges (spin $\tfrac12$) (Q-3, exact).

*Chirality.* Because $D_{\rm LL}$ is complex-linear in its argument and $K^j_{\rm LL}=iJ^j_{\rm
LL}$, every boost acts as $i$ times the corresponding rotation on every bi-octonion, $D_{\rm LL}(K^j_{\rm LL})=i\,D_{\rm LL}(J^j_{\rm LL})$ (Q-4). A Lorentz multiplet on which the rotations act with spin $\tfrac12$ (or 1) and the boosts as $i$ times the rotations is said to be of type $(\tfrac12,0)$ (or $(1,0)$); with $-i$ in place of $i$ it is of type $(0,\tfrac12)$ (or $(0,1)$). So the spinor charges are of type $(\tfrac12,0)$ and the Lorentz part of type $(1,0)$: the super-loop has one chirality. The complex-conjugate super-loop, in which every coefficient is replaced by its complex conjugate, has the other.

*Spinor number.* The rotation by the angle $2\pi$ about the third axis, $\exp(2\pi D_{\rm
LL}(J^3_{\rm LL}))$, is $+1$ on $\mathbb C\otimes\mathbb H$ and $-1$ on the spinor charges, because $iD_{\rm LL}(J^3_{\rm LL})$ has the integer eigenvalues $-1,0,0,1$ on the first and the eigenvalues $\pm\tfrac12$ on the second (Q-5): the spinor charges are fermionic in their Lorentz quantum numbers, although they are elements of an algebra with a commutator.

*Twist charge.* $\Theta_{\rm LL}$, Sec. IV, is 1 on $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$ and $-2$ on $\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}$. It is a new quantity: it measures by how much the inner action of the Lorentz part fails to be a representation, Eq. (13), and Theorem M will show that $0$, $1$ and $-2$ are the only values that a Malcev algebra allows. Its value $-2$ appears directly in the loop: conjugation by an element $\mathbf g$ of $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ acts on a spinor charge as $$\mathbf g(\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4)\bar{\mathbf g}=(\mathbf b\,\mathbf g^2)\,\mathbf e_4
\tag{17}$$ (Q-6; both orders of the two products agree), whereas the automorphism (16) acts as $\mathbf b\mapsto\mathbf b\bar{\mathbf g}=\mathbf b\,\mathbf g^{-1}$: the loop’s own conjugation turns a spinor charge twice as fast and in the opposite sense.

![image](../figures/../figures/fig_sl_charges.pdf.png)

Quantum numbers of the three sectors of $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$ under the operators of Sec. V: the electric charge $Q_{\rm LL}$, the isospin Casimir, the spin Casimir $\mathrm{Cas}_{\rm LL}$, the Lorentz type, the spinor number $\exp(2\pi D_{\rm LL}(J^3_{\rm LL}))$ and the twist charge $\Theta_{\rm LL}$ (checks Q-1 to Q-6, L-7).

### C. The inversion element

The unit $\mathbf e_4$ itself is an element of the super-loop with $\mathbf e_4^2=-\mathbf e_0$ and $\mathbf e_4^{-1}=-\mathbf e_4$. Conjugation by it acts on the bi-quaternions by $$\mathbf e_4\,\mathbf a\,\mathbf e_4^{-1}=\bar{\mathbf a}
\tag{18}$$ (C-1). On $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ this is the inversion $\mathbf g\mapsto\bar{\mathbf g}=\mathbf g^{-1}$, and on the Minkowski space $\mathbb R^{1,3}_{\rm LL}$ of the Hermitian bi-quaternions $x^0_{\rm LL}\mathbf
e_0+i\sum_jx^j_{\rm LL}\mathbf e_j$ (Sec. VII) it is the parity $x^j_{\rm LL}\mapsto-x^j_{\rm LL}$. The union of $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ and $SL(2,\mathbb C)\,\mathbf e_4$ is closed under the product, by Eq. (4): $$\begin{aligned}
(\mathbf g\,\mathbf e_4)(\mathbf h\,\mathbf e_4)&=-\bar{\mathbf h}\mathbf g,\qquad \mathbf g(\mathbf h\,\mathbf e_4)=(\mathbf h\mathbf g)\,\mathbf e_4,\\
(\mathbf g\,\mathbf e_4)\mathbf h&=(\mathbf g\bar{\mathbf h})\,\mathbf e_4
\tag{19}
\end{aligned}$$ (C-2). It is a Moufang loop of the type constructed by Chein ([Chein 1974](#ref-Chein1974)), a “double” of the Lorentz group, and it is not associative because $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ is not commutative.

Such an element cannot exist in a group. In a group, conjugation by an element is an automorphism, and the inversion $\mathbf g\mapsto\mathbf g^{-1}$ of $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ is not one: for $\mathbf g=\mathbf e_1$ and $\mathbf h=\mathbf e_2$, $(\mathbf g\mathbf h)^{-1}=-\mathbf e_3$ but $\mathbf g^{-1}\mathbf
h^{-1}=+\mathbf e_3$ (C-3). In the loop, conjugation by $\mathbf e_4$ is not an automorphism of $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$ (C-4), and the inversion is realized by an element. This is a third way in which the super-loop goes beyond what H-grp allows.

## VI. Uniqueness: the classification of covariant brackets

### A. The three families

Is the super-loop an accident of the octonions, or the only way to let spinor charges close into Lorentz generators? We answer the question for every bracket that is covariant under the Lorentz group $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ and the isospin group $SU(2)_{\rm v}$, with the spinor charges a Weyl spinor that is an isodoublet, in three families:

- $\mathcal F^{\rm A}_{\rm LL}$ on $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}\oplus\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}$ (real dimension 14);

- $\mathcal F^{\rm B}_{\rm LL}$ on $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}\oplus\mathfrak p_{\rm LL}\oplus\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}$ (18), with the translations $\mathfrak p_{\rm LL}$, the Hermitian bi-quaternions $\mathbf r=\mathbf
  r^\dagger$, on which $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$ acts by $[\mathbf u,\mathbf r]=\mathbf u\mathbf r+\mathbf
  r\mathbf u^\dagger$ (the Lorentz action on four-vectors);

- $\mathcal F^{\rm C}_{\rm LL}$ on $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}\oplus\mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak s_{\rm
  LL}$ (17), with isospin generators $\mathbf t\in{\rm Im}\,\mathbb H$ as elements.

The bracket of $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$ with itself is the Lorentz bracket, and the other brackets are $$\begin{aligned}
&=c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}(W_{\rm LL}(\mathbf u)\mathbf b)\,\mathbf e_4,\\
[\mathbf r,\mathbf r']&=c^{\rm PP}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf r\bar{\mathbf r}'-\mathbf r'\bar{\mathbf r}),\\
[\mathbf r,\mathbf b\mathbf e_4]&=c^{\rm Ps}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf b^*\bar{\mathbf r})\,\mathbf e_4,\\
[\mathbf t,\mathbf t']&=c^{\rm TT}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf t\mathbf t'-\mathbf t'\mathbf t),\tag{20}\\
[\mathbf t,\mathbf b\mathbf e_4]&=c^{\rm Ts}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf t\mathbf b)\,\mathbf e_4,\\
[\mathbf b\mathbf e_4,\mathbf b'\mathbf e_4]&=c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}(\bar{\mathbf b}\mathbf b'-\bar{\mathbf b}'\mathbf b)\\
&\quad+c^{\rm sP}_{\rm LL}\,i(\bar{\mathbf b}\mathbf b'^*-\bar{\mathbf b}'\mathbf b^*)\\
&\quad+\mathrm{Re}\big[c^{\rm sT}_{\rm LL}(\mathbf b\bar{\mathbf b}'-\mathbf b'\bar{\mathbf b})\big],
\end{aligned}$$ where a term is present only if its target sector is ($\mathfrak p_{\rm LL}$ in $\mathcal F^{\rm B}_{\rm
LL}$, $\mathfrak{su}(2)$ in $\mathcal F^{\rm C}_{\rm LL}$), the real part in the last term is taken coefficient by coefficient so that the result lies in ${\rm Im}\,\mathbb H$, $c^{\rm sP}_{\rm LL}$ and $c^{\rm TT}_{\rm LL}$ are real, and the other coefficients are complex. The bi-octonions are $\mathcal F^{\rm A}_{\rm LL}$ with $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}=-2$, $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}=1$ (Eqs. (11) and (12); check M-1).

*These brackets are the most general covariant ones.* Complexify: the Lorentz algebra becomes two commuting copies of $\mathfrak{sl}(2)$, and a complex representation is labelled by its two spins. The spinor charges are $(\tfrac12,0)$ times an isodoublet, their complex conjugates $(0,\tfrac12)$, the Lorentz part $(1,0)$ with its conjugate $(0,1)$, the translations $(\tfrac12,\tfrac12)$ and the isospin generators $(0,0)$ times an isotriplet. A covariant real bilinear bracket is a sum of covariant complex-bilinear maps between these representations and their conjugates, and the number of such maps is the number of times the target occurs in the tensor product of the sources: the target $(\tfrac12,0)$ occurs once in $(1,0)\otimes(\tfrac12,0)$ and never in $(0,1)\otimes(\tfrac12,0)$ \[the twist $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}$\]; $(1,0)$ times the isosinglet occurs once in the antisymmetric square of $(\tfrac12,0)$ times the isodoublet, and never in $(\tfrac12,0)\otimes(0,\tfrac12)$ \[$c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}$\]; the antisymmetric square of $(\tfrac12,\tfrac12)$ is $(1,0)\oplus(0,1)$ \[$c^{\rm PP}_{\rm LL}$\]; $(\tfrac12,0)$ occurs once in $(\tfrac12,\tfrac12)\otimes(0,\tfrac12)$ and never in $(\tfrac12,\tfrac12)\otimes(\tfrac12,0)$, so the bracket of a translation with a spinor charge is antilinear in the spinor \[$c^{\rm Ps}_{\rm LL}$\]; $(\tfrac12,\tfrac12)$ times the isosinglet occurs once in $(\tfrac12,0)\otimes(0,\tfrac12)$ times isodoublet $\otimes$ isodoublet, and only one real combination is antisymmetric \[$c^{\rm sP}_{\rm LL}$\]; the isotriplet occurs once in the antisymmetric square of the spinor charges (spin 0, antisymmetric, times isospin 1, symmetric) \[$c^{\rm sT}_{\rm LL}$\]; and the isodoublet occurs once in isotriplet $\otimes$ isodoublet \[$c^{\rm Ts}_{\rm LL}$\]. All other brackets between different sectors vanish by covariance. Each complex coefficient counts two real ones, as the reality of the bracket requires.

### B. Method

The Jacobi identity and the linearized Malcev identity (8) are multilinear, so they hold for all elements if they hold for all basis elements. Evaluated on integer elements, each component of an identity is a polynomial in the real and imaginary parts of the coefficients (degree 2 for Jacobi, 3 for Malcev). The script `malcev_groebner.py` evaluates the identities of each family on random integer tuples until the linear span of the resulting polynomials stops growing, and computes the reduced Gröbner basis ([Cox, Little, and O’Shea 2015](#ref-CoxLittleOShea2015)) of the ideal they generate, $\mathcal I^{\rm Jac}_{\rm LL}$ for Jacobi and $\mathcal I^{\rm Mal}_{\rm LL}$ for Malcev (Table II). Every polynomial found is a true consequence of the identity, so a polynomial that lies in the ideal vanishes on every solution: the statements below are necessary conditions, proved by ideal membership. To restrict to solutions on which a polynomial does not vanish (for example $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}\neq0$), the ideal is enlarged by that polynomial times an auxiliary variable $w^{\rm sat}_{\rm LL}$, minus 1 (saturation). Conversely, the Rust command `malcev` checks every solution found here on *all* basis triples and 4-tuples, in exact integer arithmetic, so the solutions are proved to satisfy the identities (checks M-1 to M-9).

| family                                  | identity | polynomials | tuples | basis |
|:----------------------------------------|:--------:|:-----------:|:------:|:-----:|
| $\mathcal F^{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{LL}}$ |  Jacobi  |      6      |   13   |   5   |
| $\mathcal F^{\mathrm{A}}_{\mathrm{LL}}$ |  Malcev  |      8      |   13   |   9   |
| $\mathcal F^{\mathrm{B}}_{\mathrm{LL}}$ |  Jacobi  |     19      |   15   |  19   |
| $\mathcal F^{\mathrm{B}}_{\mathrm{LL}}$ |  Malcev  |     85      |   20   |  20   |
| $\mathcal F^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{LL}}$ |  Jacobi  |     12      |   15   |  25   |
| $\mathcal F^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{LL}}$ |  Malcev  |     43      |   16   |  113  |

The constraint ideals: number of independent polynomials, of random integer tuples used, and of elements of the reduced Gröbner basis (grevlex order), for each family and identity.

### C. Theorem M

*Theorem M.* (a) In $\mathcal F^{\rm A}_{\rm LL}$, the Jacobi identity forces $c^{\rm
sl}_{\rm LL}=0$ and $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}\in\{0,1\}$ (G-1, G-2; Theorem L). (b) The Malcev identity forces $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}(c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}-1)(c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}+2)=0$, a cubic whose only roots are $0$, $1$ and $-2$ (G-3); all three give Malcev algebras, the first two Lie algebras (M-2): the value $-2$ is Carlsson’s non-Lie Malcev module of $\mathfrak{sl}(2)$ ([Carlsson 1976](#ref-Carlsson1976); [Elduque 1990](#ref-Elduque1990)). If $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}\neq0$, then $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}=-2$ (G-4), and the rescaling $\mathbf
b\mapsto\lambda^{\rm sc}_{\rm LL}\mathbf b$ by a complex number, which maps $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}$ to $(\lambda^{\rm sc}_{\rm LL})^2c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}$, makes $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}=1$: the algebra is $\mathfrak m_{\rm LL}$, the commutator algebra of the bi-octonions (M-1). Every other member fails (M-3). (c) In $\mathcal F^{\rm B}_{\rm LL}$, even the Malcev identity forces $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}=0$: with translations present, spinor charges never close into Lorentz generators (G-7). The only non-Lie Malcev member is $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}=-2$ with all other coefficients zero (G-8, G-9, M-4, M-7): spinor charges that commute with each other and with the translations, with the twisted Lorentz action. The Lie members are (i) the Poincaré algebra with commuting spinor charges, (ii) the Poincaré algebra with $[\mathfrak s_{\rm LL},\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}]\subset\mathfrak p_{\rm LL}$ ($c^{\rm sP}_{\rm LL}\neq0$), and (iii) for $c^{\rm PP}_{\rm LL}=\lvert c^{\rm Ps}_{\rm LL}\rvert^2>0$ the de Sitter algebra $\mathfrak{so}(4,1)$ (signature of its Killing form: 4 positive, 6 negative; M-6) acting on its real spinor representation, with $[\mathfrak p_{\rm LL},\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}]\neq0$ and $[\mathfrak s_{\rm
LL},\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}]=0$ (G-5, G-6, M-5). (d) In $\mathcal F^{\rm C}_{\rm LL}$, the Jacobi identity forces $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}=0$ (G-10); and if $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}\neq0$, the Malcev identity forces $c^{\rm Ts}_{\rm LL}=c^{\rm sT}_{\rm LL}=0$ and $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}=-2$ (G-11, G-12, M-8, M-9): isospin cannot act on the spinor charges as an element of the algebra; it acts on the super-loop only from outside, as the automorphisms (16).

The reduced Gröbner bases of $\mathcal F^{\rm A}_{\rm LL}$ illustrate the proof (variables: the real and imaginary parts `ctw_re`, `ctw_im`, `csl_re`, `csl_im` of $c^{\rm
tw}_{\rm LL}$ and $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}$). For the Jacobi identity the basis contains `csl_re` and `csl_im` themselves—so $c^{\rm sl}_{\rm LL}=0$ on every solution—together with the real and imaginary parts of $c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}(c^{\rm tw}_{\rm LL}-1)$. Case (ii) of (c) is a bosonic analogue of supersymmetry: spinor charges whose brackets are translations. It is a Lie algebra, so the theorem of Coleman and Mandula applies to it if it is to be a symmetry of a scattering matrix, and Haag, Łopuszański and Sohnius showed that the anticommutator, not the commutator, is the consistent choice there. Case (iii) lives in de Sitter space, where the hypotheses H-dyn on a scattering matrix are not available. Neither is a counterexample in the sense of this paper; they are listed to make the classification complete.

![image](../figures/../figures/fig_sl_classification.pdf.png)

Theorem M: what the bracket of two spinor charges can contain, for Lie algebras (top) and for non-Lie Malcev algebras (bottom), in the three families. Green: the super-loop $\mathfrak m_{\rm LL}$, unique; orange: the abelian twisted module of the Poincaré algebra; yellow: isospin decouples. The labels are those of the checks.

## VII. Manifolds that carry the super-loop

The super-loop is itself a manifold, and it acts on others. Figure 5 collects the manifolds that we identify; each statement is a machine check.

*The $7{+}1$ Minkowski space.* The Hermitian bi-octonions, $\mathbf x^\dagger=\mathbf x$, are the elements $\mathbf x=x^0_{\rm LL}\mathbf e_0+i\sum_{p=1}^7x^p_{\rm LL}\mathbf e_p$ with real coordinates $x^p_{\rm LL}$. On them the norm is real, $$N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf x)=(x^0_{\rm LL})^2-\textstyle\sum_{p=1}^7(x^p_{\rm LL})^2 ,
\tag{21}$$ with one positive and seven negative signs: they form the Minkowski space $\mathbb R^{1,7}_{\rm LL}$ of $7{+}1$ dimensions (N-1). The Hermitian bi-quaternions ($p\le3$) form the Minkowski space $\mathbb
R^{1,3}_{\rm LL}$ of $3+1$ dimensions, on which $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ acts by $\mathbf x\mapsto\mathbf
g\mathbf x\mathbf g^\dagger$: the familiar action of the Lorentz group on four-vectors. The conjugations $\mathbf x\mapsto\mathbf g\mathbf x\mathbf g^\dagger$ of $\mathbb R^{1,7}_{\rm LL}$ by the one-parameter families $\mathbf g=\exp(\theta_{\rm LL}\mathbf e_p)$ (rotations) and $\mathbf
g=\exp(\theta_{\rm LL}i\mathbf e_p)$ (boosts) of loop elements, $p=1,\dots,7$, have the generators $\mathbf x\mapsto[\mathbf e_p,\mathbf x]$ and $\mathbf x\mapsto i(\mathbf e_p\mathbf x+\mathbf x\mathbf
e_p)$; they preserve Eq. (21), and the Lie algebra they generate is all of $\mathfrak{so}(1,7)$, of dimension 28 (N-4). (The products are unambiguous: by Artin’s theorem the subalgebra generated by $\mathbf e_p$ and $\mathbf x$ is associative.)

*Conjugation by the loop is not a group action.* Restricted to the loop’s own Lorentz subgroup ($p=1,2,3$), the six generators preserve $\mathbb R^{1,3}_{\rm LL}$ and act on it as $\mathfrak{so}(3,1)$, but on $\mathbb R^{1,7}_{\rm LL}$ they generate a Lie algebra of dimension $9=6+3$ (N-5): on the four directions $i\mathbf e_4,\dots,i\mathbf e_7$ the boosts act trivially and the rotations by the twisted action of Eq. (11), and the commutators separate an extra $\mathfrak{so}(3)$ that acts on these directions alone. A group action would give back a Lie algebra of dimension 6. This is the geometric face of the twist charge.

*The $4{+}4$ manifold.* The real subspace $\mathbb O^{\rm s}_{\rm LL}=\mathbb H+(i\mathbb H)\mathbf
e_4$ of $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$ is closed under the product, and the map from the split Cayley-Dickson algebra (pairs of real quaternions with the sign $+1$ in place of the $-1$ of Eq. (4)) that sends the pair $(\mathbf a,\mathbf b)$ to $\mathbf a+(i\mathbf b)\mathbf e_4$ is an isomorphism onto it (N-2): $\mathbb O^{\rm s}_{\rm LL}$ is the algebra of *split octonions*, and the norm has on it the signature $(4,4)$. Its unit elements form the pseudo-sphere $S^{3,4}_{\rm LL}$, a Moufang loop and the $4{+}4$ real form of the super-loop. Inside its imaginary part, $\mathbf e_1$, $i\mathbf e_4$ and $i\mathbf e_5$ span the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb R)=\mathfrak{so}(2,1)$ (Killing form: two positive, one negative; N-9): on the real $4{+}4$ manifold the Lorentz part of the super-loop is the Lorentz group of $2+1$ dimensions, $SL(2,\mathbb R)=Spin(2,1)$, and $SO(3,1)$ needs the complex form. The space of spinor charges is a second $4{+}4$ space: $\mathrm{Re}\,N_{\rm LL}$ and $\mathrm{Im}\,N_{\rm
LL}$ are, on $\mathfrak s_{\rm LL}$, two quadratic forms of signature $(4,4)$, both invariant under the automorphisms (16) (N-3).

*The super-loop manifold.* $\mathbb L_{\rm LL}$, Eq. (7), is a complex quadric of real dimension 14; as a homogeneous space it is $Spin(7,\mathbb C)/\mathrm G_2(\mathbb C)$. It is *parallelizable*: the vector fields $X^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf x}(\mathbf w)=\mathbf w\mathbf x$ for the 14 real imaginary directions $\mathbf x$ are tangent to it, $N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf w,\mathbf w\mathbf x)=0$, and form a frame at every point. Their Lie brackets are $$(\mathbf w)=\mathbf w[\mathbf x,\mathbf y]+2[\mathbf w,\mathbf x,\mathbf y]
\tag{22}$$ (N-7; it follows from $(\mathbf w\mathbf x)\mathbf y-(\mathbf w\mathbf y)\mathbf x$ and alternativity). At the identity this is the Malcev bracket; elsewhere the associator makes the structure functions depend on the point. On a Lie group the frame fields of the group’s own multiplication have constant structure functions: $\mathbb L_{\rm LL}$ is a parallelizable manifold that carries no Lie group structure compatible with its product. Its compact real form, the unit octonions, is the seven-sphere $S^7_{\rm LL}$; the products $L^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf e_p}L^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf e_{p'}}$ ($p<p'$) span $\mathfrak{spin}(7)$, of dimension 21, whose stabilizer of $\mathbf e_0$ is $\mathfrak g_2$ (14), so that $S^7_{\rm LL}=Spin(7)/\mathrm G_2$ (N-8) ([Baez 2002](#ref-Baez2002)).

*The linear action closes into Lie groups.* The left multiplications by the imaginary bi-octonions generate $\mathfrak{so}(8,\mathbb C)$ (real dimension 56), those by the imaginary octonions $\mathfrak{so}(8)$, and those by the imaginary split octonions on $\mathbb O^{\rm s}_{\rm LL}$ $\mathfrak{so}(4,4)$ (N-6). Any *linear* action of the loop on a vector space generates a Lie group, in agreement with Theorem R; the non-associative structure lives in the product of the loop, not in its linear action. Section X uses this fact.

![image](../figures/../figures/fig_sl_manifolds.pdf.png)

The manifolds that carry the super-loop: $\mathbb R^{1,7}_{\rm LL}$ and $\mathbb R^{1,3}_{\rm
LL}$ (conjugation), the split octonions $\mathbb O^{\rm s}_{\rm LL}$ ($4{+}4$), the space of spinor charges with its two $(4,4)$ forms, the super-loop manifold and its compact real form $S^7_{\rm LL}$. The labels are those of the checks.

## VIII. The counterexamples

We collect the counterexamples to the conclusion of the no-go theorems, each with the hypothesis that fails and the reason why it must fail.

*Counterexample 1: the Lorentz super-loop.* $\mathbb L_{\rm LL}$ contains the Lorentz group $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ and eight spinor charges that carry internal quantum numbers—isospin $\tfrac12$ and electric charge $\pm\tfrac12$—and Lorentz quantum numbers—spin $\tfrac12$, one chirality, spinor number $-1$. The bracket of two spinor charges is a Lorentz generator, Eq. (12), so the structure is not a direct product of a Lorentz part and an internal part. For a Lie algebra this is impossible (Theorem L); for a non-associative structure it happens, and uniquely (Theorem M(b)). Hypotheses that fail: H-grp (the loop is not associative), H-op and H-Lie (Theorem R: there is no realization by operators, and the Jacobi identity fails, Eq. (6)).

*Counterexample 2: inversion as an element.* The unit $\mathbf e_4$ of the super-loop acts on $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ by the inversion and on $\mathbb R^{1,3}_{\rm LL}$ by the parity, Eq. (18), and together with $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ it generates a Moufang loop, Eq. (19). No group contains such an element (check C-3). Hypothesis that fails: H-grp.

*Counterexample 3: a parallelizable manifold of Lorentz and spinor directions without a group structure.* The super-loop manifold carries frame fields along the six Lorentz and the eight spinor directions whose brackets, Eq. (22), mix the two kinds and depend on the point through the associator. Its real forms, $S^7_{\rm LL}$ and the pseudo-sphere $S^{3,4}_{\rm LL}$ of the $4{+}4$ manifold, have the same property. Hypothesis that fails: H-grp (and H-Lie for the frames).

*Counterexample 4, weaker: spinor charges of the Poincaré algebra with a twisted Lorentz action.* With translations present, no Malcev algebra of our families lets spinor charges close into Lorentz generators (Theorem M(c)); the only non-Lie extension is the abelian module on which the Lorentz generators act with the twist $-2$: spinor charges that commute with the translations and with each other but transform under a Lorentz action that is not a representation (check M-4). Its untwisted version is a Lie algebra, which Coleman and Mandula exclude as a symmetry of a scattering matrix through H-op and H-dyn; the twisted version escapes H-Lie as well, but, having no operator realization, it is not a symmetry of a scattering matrix of an ordinary quantum theory either.

What none of these is: a symmetry of the scattering matrix of a relativistic quantum field theory on a Hilbert space. Theorem R forbids it. The counterexamples show which conclusions of the no-go theorems rest on associativity alone: the separation of Lorentz and internal generators (Theorem L versus Counterexample 1) and the realization of every element by an automorphism (Counterexample 2).

## IX. The spin-zero superfluid of the spinor charges

### A. The field content from the bi-octonion product

Write a spinor charge $\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4$ through the $2\times2$ matrix $M_{\rm LL}(\mathbf b)$ of Sec. III. The Lorentz derivation (14) multiplies $\mathbf b$ by $-\mathbf u$ from the right, which acts within each row of $M_{\rm LL}(\mathbf b)$; the isospin derivation (15) multiplies from the left, which mixes the two rows. Define the $2\times2$ matrix $$\psi'_{\rm LL}=\mathsf E_{\rm LL}^{-1}M_{\rm LL}(\mathbf b)^T,\qquad \mathsf E_{\rm LL}=i\sigma^{\rm LL}_2 .
\tag{23}$$ Its first column (isospin up) and its second column (isospin down) are Weyl spinors: on each column the Lorentz derivations act as $D_{\rm LL}(J^j_{\rm LL})=-\tfrac{i}{2}\sigma^{\rm LL}_j$ and $D_{\rm
LL}(K^j_{\rm LL})=+\tfrac12\sigma^{\rm LL}_j$, and the isospin generators $T^\iota_{\rm LL}$ act on the column index as $\tfrac12\tau^{\rm LL}_\iota$ (check S-0; the step uses $\mathsf E_{\rm
LL}^{-1}(\sigma^{\rm LL}_j)^T\mathsf E_{\rm LL}=-\sigma^{\rm LL}_j$).

In the chiral basis the Dirac matrices are $\gamma^0_{\rm LL}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\sigma^{\rm
LL}_0\\\sigma^{\rm LL}_0&0\end{pmatrix}$, $\gamma^j_{\rm LL}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\sigma^{\rm
LL}_j\\-\sigma^{\rm LL}_j&0\end{pmatrix}$, with the chirality matrix $\gamma^{\rm ch}_{\rm LL}=i\gamma^0_{\rm
LL}\gamma^1_{\rm LL}\gamma^2_{\rm LL}\gamma^3_{\rm LL}=\mathrm{diag}(-1,-1,1,1)$ and the charge-conjugation matrix $C_{\rm LL}=i\gamma^2_{\rm LL}\gamma^0_{\rm LL}$ ([Peskin and Schroeder 1995](#ref-PeskinSchroeder1995)). The spinor charges, on which $D_{\rm LL}(K^j_{\rm LL})=+\tfrac12\sigma^{\rm LL}_j$, are the lower block ($\gamma^{\rm ch}_{\rm
LL}=+1$); the complex-conjugate super-loop, on which the boosts act with the opposite sign, supplies the upper block. The Dirac matrices intertwine these spinor generators with the action of the loop’s conjugations on $\mathbb R^{1,3}_{\rm LL}$: for each of the six generators, $[\gamma^p_{\rm LL},\cdot\,]$ reproduces the vector action $\mathcal V_{\rm LL}$ (all 24 relations of S-0). The field of the spinor charges is therefore a *Dirac isodoublet* $\Psi_{\rm LL}$ with 8 components (Dirac $\otimes$ isospin), with electric charges $Q_{\rm LL}=\pm\tfrac12$. Its components have spinor number $-1$, so, by the spin-statistics theorem of relativistic quantum field theory, $\Psi_{\rm LL}$ is a fermion field: its components anticommute.

The Hamiltonian is that of free Dirac particles of mass $m_{\rm LL}$ at the chemical potential $\mu_{\rm
LL}$, with an attractive contact interaction in the spin-zero channels of Theorem SF1 below, $$\mathcal H^{\rm sf}_{\rm LL}=\sum_{\vec q_{\rm LL}}\Psi^\dagger_{\rm LL}(\mathsf h_{\rm LL}-\mu_{\rm LL})\Psi_{\rm LL}-\tfrac14G_{\rm LL}\sum_\iota(\Pi^\iota_{\rm LL})^\dagger\Pi^\iota_{\rm LL},
\tag{24}$$ with $\mathsf h_{\rm LL}(\vec q_{\rm LL})=\sum_j\alpha^j_{\rm LL}q^j_{\rm LL}+\beta_{\rm LL}m_{\rm LL}$ (times $\sigma^{\rm LL}_0$ on the isospin index), $\alpha^j_{\rm LL}=\gamma^0_{\rm LL}\gamma^j_{\rm LL}$, $\beta_{\rm LL}=\gamma^0_{\rm LL}$, and the pair operators $\Pi^\iota_{\rm LL}=\sum_{\vec q_{\rm
LL}}\Psi_{\rm LL}(\vec q_{\rm LL})^T\mathsf K^\iota_{\rm LL}\Psi_{\rm LL}(-\vec q_{\rm LL})$, which annihilate two spinor charges of opposite momenta. Momentum sums run over $q_{\rm LL}<\Lambda_{\rm LL}$.

### B. The spin-zero channels

*Theorem SF1.* The antisymmetric $8\times8$ matrices $\mathsf K$ for which the pair $\Psi^T_{\rm LL}\mathsf K\Psi_{\rm LL}$ is Lorentz invariant form a complex space of dimension 6, spanned by $$\mathsf K^\iota_{\rm LL}=C_{\rm LL}\gamma^{\rm ch}_{\rm LL}\otimes\mathsf E_{\rm LL}\tau^{\rm LL}_\iota,\quad \mathsf K'^\iota_{\rm LL}=C_{\rm LL}\otimes\mathsf E_{\rm LL}\tau^{\rm LL}_\iota
\tag{25}$$ ($\iota=1,2,3$; scalar $0^+$ and pseudoscalar $0^-$). Every spin-zero s-wave pair of spinor charges is an isospin *triplet*, with the electric charges $+1$, $0$, $-1$.

*Proof.* The Lorentz-invariant bilinear forms of a Dirac spinor are those of $(\tfrac12,0)\otimes(\tfrac12,0)$ and $(0,\tfrac12)\otimes(0,\tfrac12)$, one each, spanned by $C_{\rm
LL}=\mathrm{diag}(\mathsf E_{\rm LL},-\mathsf E_{\rm LL})$ and $C_{\rm LL}\gamma^{\rm ch}_{\rm
LL}=-\mathrm{diag}(\mathsf E_{\rm LL},\mathsf E_{\rm LL})$; both are antisymmetric. For anticommuting components only the antisymmetric part of $\mathsf K$ contributes, so the isospin factor must be symmetric. The symmetric $2\times2$ matrices are spanned by the three matrices $\mathsf E_{\rm
LL}\tau^{\rm LL}_\iota$, which transform as an isospin vector; the remaining matrix $\mathsf E_{\rm LL}$, the isosinglet, is antisymmetric and drops out. $\square$ (Check S-1 computes the dimension 6 directly and the isospin Casimir 2 of every channel.) The pair $\mathsf K^3_{\rm LL}$ joins an isospin-up with an isospin-down charge (charge 0), and $\mathsf K^1_{\rm LL}\mp i\mathsf K^2_{\rm LL}$ joins two up or two down charges (charge $\pm1$) (S-2).

### C. Mean field and spectra

The order parameter is the complex isospin vector $\vec d_{\rm LL}$ with $d^\iota_{\rm
LL}=-\tfrac12G_{\rm LL}\langle\Pi^\iota_{\rm LL}\rangle$. Replacing the product of pair operators in Eq. (24) by its mean-field form gives, for each momentum, the Bogoliubov-de Gennes matrix $\mathsf H_{\rm LL}$ on the Nambu spinor $(\Psi_{\rm LL}(\vec q_{\rm LL}),\Psi^*_{\rm LL}(-\vec q_{\rm LL}))$, $$\mathsf H_{\rm LL}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf h_{\rm LL}(\vec q_{\rm LL})-\mu_{\rm LL}&\mathsf P_{\rm LL}\\\mathsf P_{\rm LL}^\dagger&-(\mathsf h_{\rm LL}(-\vec q_{\rm LL})-\mu_{\rm LL})^T\end{pmatrix},
\tag{26}$$ with $\mathsf P_{\rm LL}=\sum_\iota d^\iota_{\rm LL}(\mathsf K^\iota_{\rm LL})^\dagger$, and the grand potential per volume at zero temperature, measured from the normal state, $$\begin{aligned}
\Omega_{\rm LL}={}&\frac{\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\rvert^2}{G_{\rm LL}}-\frac12\int\!\frac{d^3q_{\rm LL}}{(2\pi)^3}\sum_{\rm pos}\big(\text{eigenvalues of }\mathsf H_{\rm LL}\big)\\
&-(\ldots)_{\vec d_{\rm LL}=0},
\tag{27}
\end{aligned}$$ the sum running over the positive eigenvalues.

*Theorem SF2.* In the scalar channel the positive eigenvalues of $\mathsf H_{\rm LL}$ are $E^+_{\rm LL}$ and $E^-_{\rm LL}$ for each of the two gaps $$(\Delta^\pm_{\rm LL})^2=\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\rvert^2(1\pm\zeta_{\rm LL}),\qquad \zeta_{\rm LL}=\frac{\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\times\vec d^{\,*}_{\rm LL}\rvert}{\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\rvert^2},
\tag{28}$$ each twice, where $E^\pm_{\rm LL}=[(\varepsilon_{\rm LL}\pm\mu_{\rm LL})^2+\Delta_{\rm LL}^2]^{1/2}$ with $\Delta_{\rm LL}$ replaced by the gap, and $\varepsilon_{\rm LL}=(q_{\rm LL}^2+m_{\rm LL}^2)^{1/2}$. A state with $\zeta_{\rm LL}=0$ is called *unitary*, as in the theory of superfluid $^3$He ([Balian and Werthamer 1963](#ref-BalianWerthamer1963); [Leggett 1975](#ref-Leggett1975)); it has the single gap $\Delta_{\rm LL}=\lvert\vec d_{\rm
LL}\rvert$. (The scalar channel $C_{\rm LL}\gamma^{\rm ch}_{\rm LL}$ is also the Dirac structure of the pairs of color-superconducting quark matter ([Alford, Rajagopal, and Wilczek 1999](#ref-AlfordRajagopalWilczek1999)).)

*Proof.* Write $\mathsf P_{\rm LL}=(C_{\rm LL}\gamma^{\rm ch}_{\rm LL})^\dagger\otimes\mathsf
D_{\rm LL}$ with the isospin matrix $\mathsf D_{\rm LL}=\sum_\iota d^\iota_{\rm LL}(\mathsf E_{\rm
LL}\tau^{\rm LL}_\iota)^\dagger$. Then $\mathsf D_{\rm LL}\mathsf D_{\rm LL}^\dagger=\lvert\vec d_{\rm
LL}\rvert^2\sigma^{\rm LL}_0+i\sum_\iota(\vec d_{\rm LL}\times\vec d^{\,*}_{\rm LL})^\iota\tau^{\rm LL}_\iota$, whose eigenvalues are $(\Delta^\pm_{\rm LL})^2$, because $i\,\vec d_{\rm LL}\times\vec d^{\,*}_{\rm LL}$ is a real vector. The unitary matrix $C_{\rm LL}\gamma^{\rm ch}_{\rm LL}$ maps $\mathsf h_{\rm LL}(-\vec q_{\rm
LL})^T$ to $\mathsf h_{\rm LL}(\vec q_{\rm LL})$ by conjugation and has the square $-\mathsf 1_4$; a unitary change of basis in the hole block therefore turns $\mathsf H_{\rm LL}$ into the matrix with the blocks $\mathsf h_{\rm LL}-\mu_{\rm LL}$, $-(\mathsf h_{\rm LL}-\mu_{\rm LL})$ on the diagonal and $\mp\mathsf
1_4\otimes\mathsf D_{\rm LL}$ off it. In the singular basis of $\mathsf D_{\rm LL}$ it splits into two blocks, one per gap, whose squares are $(\mathsf h_{\rm LL}-\mu_{\rm LL})^2+(\Delta^\pm_{\rm LL})^2$; the eigenvalues of $\mathsf h_{\rm LL}$ are $\pm\varepsilon_{\rm LL}$, twice each. $\square$ Check S-3 compares these energies with the eigenvalues of $\mathsf H_{\rm LL}$ at 60 random points (largest deviation $7.11\times10^{-15}$). In the pseudoscalar channel, $C_{\rm LL}$ maps $\mathsf h_{\rm LL}(-\vec q_{\rm
LL})^T$ to the matrix with the opposite mass, the blocks do not decouple, and a unitary state has the energies $\tilde E^\pm_{\rm LL}$ with $(\tilde E^\pm_{\rm LL})^2=\varepsilon_{\rm LL}^2+\mu_{\rm
LL}^2+\Delta_{\rm LL}^2\pm2[\mu_{\rm LL}^2\varepsilon_{\rm LL}^2+m_{\rm LL}^2\Delta_{\rm LL}^2]^{1/2}$, four times each (S-3).

### D. Gap equation and critical coupling

For a unitary scalar state, Eq. (27) is $\Omega_{\rm LL}=\Delta_{\rm LL}^2/G_{\rm LL}$ minus twice the integral of $E^+_{\rm LL}+E^-_{\rm LL}$ (each energy occurs twice among the eight positive eigenvalues, and the factor $\tfrac12$ halves the sum), measured from $\Delta_{\rm LL}=0$. Setting its derivative with respect to $\Delta_{\rm LL}^2$ to zero gives the gap equation $$\frac1{G_{\rm LL}}=\int_{q_{\rm LL}<\Lambda_{\rm LL}}\!\frac{d^3q_{\rm LL}}{(2\pi)^3}\Big(\frac1{E^+_{\rm LL}}+\frac1{E^-_{\rm LL}}\Big).
\tag{29}$$ In the vacuum ($\mu_{\rm LL}=0$) a gap appears only above the critical coupling obtained at $\Delta_{\rm LL}\to0$, $$G^{\rm crit}_{\rm LL}=\frac{2\pi^2}{\Lambda_{\rm LL}(\Lambda_{\rm LL}^2+m_{\rm LL}^2)^{1/2}-m_{\rm LL}^2\ln\frac{\Lambda_{\rm LL}+(\Lambda_{\rm LL}^2+m_{\rm LL}^2)^{1/2}}{m_{\rm LL}}},
\tag{30}$$ which is $2\pi^2/\Lambda_{\rm LL}^2={}$<!-- -->19.739209 for $m_{\rm LL}=0$ and 21.286610 for $m_{\rm LL}=0.2$ (S-4; quadrature against the closed form). With a Fermi surface ($\mu_{\rm LL}>m_{\rm
LL}$) the integral of $1/E^-_{\rm LL}$ diverges logarithmically as $\Delta_{\rm LL}\to0$, so a gap forms at every positive coupling. At weak coupling $\ln\Delta_{\rm LL}$ is linear in $1/G_{\rm LL}$ with the slope $-1/(2\nu^{\rm F}_{\rm LL})=-\pi^2/(k^{\rm F}_{\rm LL}\mu_{\rm LL})$, where $k^{\rm F}_{\rm
LL}=(\mu_{\rm LL}^2-m_{\rm LL}^2)^{1/2}$ and $\nu^{\rm F}_{\rm LL}=k^{\rm F}_{\rm LL}\mu_{\rm LL}/(2\pi^2)$ is the density of states of one species and one spin state at the Fermi surface, the law of Bardeen, Cooper and Schrieffer ([Bardeen, Cooper, and Schrieffer 1957](#ref-BCS1957)): the slope of the computed gaps is $-43.0637$ against $-43.0745$ (ratio $0.9997$; S-5; $\mu_{\rm LL}=0.5$, $m_{\rm LL}=0.2$, couplings from $6$ to $29.4$). Figure 6 shows the gap and the spectra.

### E. Which state condenses

*Theorem SF3.* At fixed $\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\rvert$, $\Omega_{\rm LL}$ is smallest for the unitary states, and the minimum over $\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\rvert$ increases with $\zeta_{\rm LL}$; the fully non-unitary state, $\zeta_{\rm LL}=1$, has exactly half the condensation energy of the unitary state.

*Proof.* The quantity $E^+_{\rm LL}+E^-_{\rm LL}$ is a concave function of $\Delta_{\rm
LL}^2$ (each term is the square root of a linear function). Because $(\Delta^\pm_{\rm LL})^2$ are $\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\rvert^2(1\pm\zeta_{\rm LL})$, whose mean is $\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\rvert^2$, Jensen’s inequality makes the sum of the energies of the two gaps at most twice the energy of the unitary state with the same $\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\rvert$, with equality only at $\zeta_{\rm LL}=0$; the energies enter $\Omega_{\rm LL}$ with a minus sign. For $\zeta_{\rm LL}=1$ one gap is zero and the other is $\sqrt2\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\rvert$; then $\Omega_{\rm LL}$ is half the unitary $\Omega_{\rm LL}$ at the gap $\sqrt2\lvert\vec d_{\rm LL}\rvert$, and so its minimum is half the unitary minimum. $\square$ At $G_{\rm LL}=12$, $\mu_{\rm LL}=0.5$, $m_{\rm LL}=0.2$ the minima are $-9.720605\times10^{-5}$ ($\zeta_{\rm LL}=0$) and $-4.860303\times10^{-5}$ ($\zeta_{\rm LL}=1$), ratio $0.500000$ (S-6).

The ground state is therefore a unitary isospin-triplet condensate, the polar state $\vec d_{\rm
LL}\propto(0,0,1)$ up to an isospin rotation. Of the four generators of $SU(2)_{\rm v}\times
U(1)^{\rm F}_{\rm LL}$ (isospin and the fermion number $F_{\rm LL}$, which multiplies $\vec d_{\rm LL}$ by $2i$) it breaks three and keeps the rotations about the third isospin axis: the electric charge $Q_{\rm
LL}=T^3_{\rm LL}$ is unbroken, and the superfluid is neutral (S-7). The charged state $\vec d_{\rm
LL}\propto(1,i,0)$, a condensate of pairs of two isospin-up charges with $Q_{\rm LL}=+1$, keeps one combination of $T^3_{\rm LL}$ and $F_{\rm LL}$ and also breaks three generators, but it is the $\zeta_{\rm LL}=1$ state of Theorem SF3 and loses. At equal coupling the scalar channel beats the pseudoscalar one for massive spinor charges: the minima of $\Omega_{\rm LL}$ are $-9.720605\times10^{-5}$ ($0^+$) and $-4.685258\times10^{-5}$ ($0^-$) at $m_{\rm LL}=0.2$, and both are $-1.384064\times10^{-4}$ at $m_{\rm
LL}=0$, where a chiral rotation maps one channel onto the other (S-10).

### F. The super-loop singlet

Two spinor charges multiply in the loop as $$(\mathbf b\,\mathbf e_4)(\mathbf b'\mathbf e_4)=-\bar{\mathbf b}'\mathbf b,
\tag{31}$$ a bi-quaternion with a scalar part, $-N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf b',\mathbf b)$, and an imaginary part in $\mathfrak l_{\rm LL}$.

*Theorem SF4.* The scalar part of Eq. (31) is invariant under the Lorentz and the isospin automorphisms—spin 0, isospin 0, charge 0, the singlet of the whole super-loop—and the imaginary part is an isospin singlet of spin 1, the bracket (12). The scalar part is *symmetric* in the two spinor charges. A field whose components anticommute therefore has no super-loop-singlet pair; it needs two species.

*Proof.* Under the automorphism (16) $\mathbf b\mapsto\mathbf v\mathbf
b\bar{\mathbf g}$, and $N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf v\mathbf b'\bar{\mathbf g},\mathbf v\mathbf b\bar{\mathbf
g})=N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf b',\mathbf b)$ because the norm is multiplicative and $N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf
v)=N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf g)=1$; the imaginary part changes to $\mathbf g(\ldots)\bar{\mathbf g}$, the adjoint action. $N_{\rm LL}(\mathbf b',\mathbf b)$ is a symmetric bilinear form, and the square of an anticommuting variable vanishes. $\square$ (S-8.) In the language of Theorem SF1, the isosinglet $C_{\rm LL}\gamma^{\rm ch}_{\rm LL}\otimes\mathsf E_{\rm LL}$ is symmetric and drops out for one field; for two species $\Psi^{(1)}_{\rm LL}$, $\Psi^{(2)}_{\rm LL}$ the matrix $\mathsf K^{\rm s}_{\rm LL}=C_{\rm
LL}\gamma^{\rm ch}_{\rm LL}\otimes\mathsf E_{\rm LL}\otimes\mathsf E_{\rm LL}$ (the last factor on the species index) is antisymmetric, as fermions require. Its condensate, a fully gapped superfluid with the energies $E^-_{\rm LL}$ and $E^+_{\rm LL}$ (eight times each; $0.347098$ and $0.878250$ at the test point of S-9), breaks only the fermion number and keeps isospin, electric charge and Lorentz invariance. This is the superfluid of spin-zero bosonic singlets in the strict sense: a singlet of the whole super-loop.

![image](../figures/../figures/fig_sl_superfluid.pdf.png)

The spin-zero superfluid of the spinor charges ($\Lambda_{\rm LL}=1$). (a) Quasi-particle energies versus momentum at $G_{\rm LL}=12$, $\mu_{\rm LL}=0.5$, $m_{\rm LL}=0.2$ (gap $0.090417$): the polar state, the gapped branch of the charged state, the normal state and the pseudoscalar channel at the same gap (Theorem SF2). (b) The gap of the unitary state versus the coupling in units of $G^{\rm crit}_{\rm LL}$ at $m_{\rm LL}=0$, Eq. (30). (c) Weak coupling: $\ln\Delta_{\rm
LL}$ versus $1/G_{\rm LL}$ and the slope of Bardeen, Cooper and Schrieffer (S-5). (d) The minimum of $\Omega_{\rm LL}$ at fixed non-unitarity $\zeta_{\rm LL}$, relative to the unitary state (Theorem SF3).

## X. Are these theories renormalizable?

A quantum field theory is *renormalizable* if the divergences of its perturbation theory can be removed by adjusting finitely many parameters of its Lagrangian. The test is power counting. In $D+1$ space-time dimensions (with $D$ the number of space dimensions) the action is dimensionless, so a fermion field has the mass dimension $[\Psi_{\rm LL}]=D/2$, a scalar field $[\phi_{\rm LL}]=(D-1)/2$, and the couplings have $$=1-D,\quad [g^{\rm Y}_{\rm LL}]=\tfrac{3-D}2,\quad [\lambda^{(1)}_{\rm LL}]=[\lambda^{(2)}_{\rm LL}]=3-D
\tag{32}$$ for the contact interaction of Eq. (24), a Yukawa coupling of a scalar to two fermions, and a quartic coupling of scalars (R-1). A coupling of negative mass dimension makes a theory non-renormalizable: for $D=3$ the contact coupling has $[G_{\rm LL}]=-2$, while the Yukawa and quartic couplings are dimensionless; for $D=7$—the $7{+}1$ manifold, and the $4{+}4$ manifold, where power counting sees the total dimension 8—all of them have negative dimension ($-6$, $-2$, $-4$).

### A. Power counting

A one-particle-irreducible graph with $n^{\rm v}_{\rm LL}$ vertices, $n^{\rm int,F}_{\rm LL}$ internal fermion and $n^{\rm int,B}_{\rm LL}$ internal boson lines has $n^{\rm loop}_{\rm LL}=n^{\rm int,F}_{\rm
LL}+n^{\rm int,B}_{\rm LL}-n^{\rm v}_{\rm LL}+1$ loops, and, since a fermion propagator falls like the inverse momentum and a boson propagator like its square, the *superficial degree of divergence* $$\deg_{\rm LL}=(D+1)\,n^{\rm loop}_{\rm LL}-n^{\rm int,F}_{\rm LL}-2\,n^{\rm int,B}_{\rm LL}.
\tag{33}$$ Eliminating the internal lines with the number of line ends at the vertices gives, for the contact theory and for the theory with Yukawa and quartic vertices, $$\begin{aligned}
\deg_{\rm LL}&=(D+1)-\tfrac D2n^{\rm ext,F}_{\rm LL}+(D-1)\,n^{\rm v}_{\rm LL},\tag{34}\\
\deg_{\rm LL}&=(D+1)-\tfrac D2n^{\rm ext,F}_{\rm LL}-\tfrac{D-1}2n^{\rm ext,B}_{\rm LL}\\
&\quad+(D-3)\big(\tfrac12n^{\rm v,Y}_{\rm LL}+n^{\rm v,4}_{\rm LL}\big),\tag{35}
\end{aligned}$$ with $n^{\rm ext,F}_{\rm LL}$, $n^{\rm ext,B}_{\rm LL}$ the external fermion and boson lines and $n^{\rm v,Y}_{\rm LL}$, $n^{\rm v,4}_{\rm LL}$ the Yukawa and quartic vertices; check R-2 compares both formulas with the direct count on all graphs with up to six vertices. For $D=3$ the degree of the contact theory grows by 2 with every vertex: infinitely many counterterms are needed. The degree of the Yukawa theory does not depend on the vertices: only the finitely many functions with $\deg_{\rm
LL}\ge0$ diverge. For $D=7$ both grow (Fig. 7(a)).

### B. The contact theory needs a cutoff

At a fixed physical gap the bare coupling that solves the gap equation (29) must vary with the cutoff as $G_{\rm LL}\Lambda_{\rm LL}^2/(2\pi^2)\to1$: the value is $0.99981$ at $\Lambda_{\rm LL}=128$ ($\Delta_{\rm LL}=0.05$, $\mu_{\rm LL}=0.5$, $m_{\rm LL}=0.2$; R-3, Fig. 7(b)). The cutoff cannot be removed by a finite number of logarithmic counterterms; like the model of Nambu and Jona-Lasinio ([Nambu and Jona-Lasinio 1961](#ref-NambuJonaLasinio1961)), the contact theory is an effective theory valid below $\Lambda_{\rm LL}$.

### C. The Yukawa completion

Replace the contact interaction by a complex isospin-triplet pair field $\phi_{\rm LL}=(\phi^1_{\rm
LL},\phi^2_{\rm LL},\phi^3_{\rm LL})$, a scalar of mass dimension 1 for $D=3$, with kinetic and mass terms, the Yukawa coupling $g^{\rm Y}_{\rm LL}$ to the pairs $\Psi^T_{\rm LL}\mathsf K^\iota_{\rm LL}\Psi_{\rm
LL}$ (plus the Hermitian conjugate), and quartic self-couplings. Below the mass of $\phi_{\rm LL}$ its exchange reproduces the contact interaction. The one-loop self-energy of $\phi_{\rm LL}$ is, up to the constant factor of the traces over the Dirac and isospin indices, the Euclidean bubble $$\begin{aligned}
I^{\rm bub}_{\rm LL}={}&\int_{k^{\rm E}_{\rm LL}<\Lambda_{\rm LL}}\!\frac{d^4k^{\rm E}_{\rm LL}}{(2\pi)^4}\\
&\times\frac{k^{\rm E}_{\rm LL}\cdot(k^{\rm E}_{\rm LL}+p^{\rm E}_{\rm LL})+m_{\rm LL}^2}{[(k^{\rm E}_{\rm LL})^2+m_{\rm LL}^2][(k^{\rm E}_{\rm LL}+p^{\rm E}_{\rm LL})^2+m_{\rm LL}^2]}
\tag{36}
\end{aligned}$$ with the loop momentum $k^{\rm E}_{\rm LL}$ and the external momentum $p^{\rm E}_{\rm LL}$. Its value at $p^{\rm E}_{\rm LL}=0$ grows like $\Lambda_{\rm LL}^2$ and is absorbed by the mass counterterm of $\phi_{\rm LL}$; its momentum dependence grows like the logarithm of the cutoff, $d[I^{\rm bub}_{\rm
LL}(p^{\rm E}_{\rm LL})-I^{\rm bub}_{\rm LL}(0)]/d\ln\Lambda_{\rm LL}\to-(p^{\rm E}_{\rm LL})^2/(16\pi^2)$, and is absorbed by the wave-function counterterm: the computed slope is $-6.332440\times10^{-3}$ against $-6.332574\times10^{-3}$ (ratio $0.99998$; $p^{\rm E}_{\rm LL}=1$, $m_{\rm LL}=0.5$; R-4, Fig. 7(c)). The list of renormalizable couplings is finite: besides $g^{\rm Y}_{\rm LL}$ and the masses, the quartic polynomials of $\phi_{\rm LL}$ invariant under isospin form a space of dimension exactly 2, spanned by $(\phi^\dagger_{\rm LL}\phi_{\rm LL})^2$ and $\lvert\phi^T_{\rm
LL}\phi_{\rm LL}\rvert^2$ (R-5), with the couplings $\lambda^{(1)}_{\rm LL}$ and $\lambda^{(2)}_{\rm LL}$.

### D. Does non-associativity change the answer?

No. A Lagrangian is built from fields on which the symmetry acts linearly, and a linear action of the loop generates a Lie group (Sec. VII). Check R-6 makes this precise for the simplest case: the quadratic forms on $\mathbb B_{\rm LL}$ that are invariant under the 14 left multiplications $L^{\rm LL}_{\mathbf x}$ and those invariant under their 56-dimensional Lie closure $\mathfrak{so}(8,\mathbb
C)$ form the same two-dimensional space, spanned by $\mathrm{Re}\,N_{\rm LL}$ and $\mathrm{Im}\,N_{\rm LL}$. Every counterterm that the closure allows the loop allows, and conversely; power counting is unchanged.

*Verdict.* In $3+1$ dimensions the spin-zero spinor superfluid of the super-loop is described by a renormalizable theory in its Yukawa form; its contact form is non-renormalizable and needs the cutoff. On the $7{+}1$ and $4{+}4$ manifolds every form is non-renormalizable. The non-associativity of the super-loop neither causes nor cures this.

![image](../figures/../figures/fig_sl_renorm.pdf.png)

Renormalizability. (a) The superficial degree of divergence of the four-fermion function of the contact theory and of the fermion self-energy of the Yukawa theory versus the number of vertices, Eqs. (34) and (35), for $D=3$ and $D=7$ (R-2). (b) The bare contact coupling at a fixed gap versus the cutoff (R-3). (c) The momentum-dependent part of the bubble (36) versus $\ln\Lambda_{\rm LL}$, with the slope $-(p^{\rm E}_{\rm LL})^2/(16\pi^2)$ (R-4).

## XI. What has been established, and what has not

### A. Established

- The algebraic facts of Secs. III to V, in exact arithmetic: the bi-octonions are alternative and not associative, their unit elements form a Moufang loop, and its tangent algebra is a Malcev algebra that is not a Lie algebra; it contains $\mathfrak{so}(3,1)$ and eight spinor charges with the quantum numbers of Fig. 3.

- Theorem R: the Malcev algebra has no non-zero realization by operators, and the loop is not a group of operators.

- Theorem L: no Lie algebra extends the Lorentz algebra by bosonic spinor charges whose brackets contain Lorentz generators.

- Theorem M: within the most general Lorentz- and isospin-covariant brackets on the sectors of the families $\mathcal F^{\rm A}_{\rm LL}$, $\mathcal F^{\rm B}_{\rm LL}$, $\mathcal F^{\rm C}_{\rm LL}$, the bi-octonion algebra is the unique Malcev algebra in which spinor charges close into Lorentz generators. The necessary conditions are proved by ideal membership in Gröbner bases (every polynomial used is a true consequence of the identity), and every solution is verified on all basis tuples in exact arithmetic.

- The manifolds of Sec. VII, and the fact that the super-loop manifold is parallelizable without a compatible Lie group structure.

- The superfluid of Sec. IX at the level of mean-field theory at zero temperature with a sharp momentum cutoff: the channels (Theorem SF1), the spectra (SF2), the gap equation and its critical coupling, the law of Bardeen, Cooper and Schrieffer, the ground state (SF3), its symmetry breaking, and the super-loop singlet (SF4).
